C01

Paper: Why Johnny Can't Prompt: How Non-AI Experts Try (and Fail) to Design LLM Prompts
Reading goal: Explore potential use in my research
Research context: I am a PhD student in NLP moving into HCI. My project studies how non-expert users write prompts for LLM-based tools, and I plan to run a user study.
Language setting: English

Turn 1

For A · previous PaperLens reply
(first message of the conversation)
Learner message
Hi, I am Kenji, PhD student in NLP and I am trying to move to HCI. Could you tell me what the main findings of this paper are? I want to know if it is useful for my project on how non-experts write prompts for LLM tools.
Move the simulated learner was given
Ask what the main findings of the paper are.
PaperLens reply (expand a source to see the cited text; highlighted = evidence quote that occurs in it)
Short answer
The paper reports that ten non-expert participants explored prompts opportunistically rather than systematically and struggled to make robust progress, with barriers coming from human-like expectations of the LLM and from over-generalizing from single observations. For your user study on non-expert prompt writing, the most directly relevant parts are the observed behaviours and the design implications it draws.
d606a330-p001-c01 · PDF p. 1 · Original passage
Ultimately, our probe participants explored prompt designs opportunistically, not systematically, and struggled in ways echoing end-user programming systems and interactive machine learning systems. Expectations stemming from human-to-human instructional experiences, and a tendency to overgeneralize, were barriers to efective prompt design. These fndings have implications for non-AI-expert-facing LLM-based tool design and for improving LLM-and-prompt literacy among programmers and the public, and present opportunities for further research. CCS CONCEPTS • Human-centered computing → Empirical studies in interaction design; • Computing methodologies → Natural language processing. KEYWORDS language models, end-users, design tools ACM Reference Format: J.D. Zamfrescu-Pereira, Richmond Wong, Bjoern Hartmann, and Qian Yang. 2023. Why Johnny Can’t Prompt: How Non-AI Experts Try (and Fail) to Design LLM Prompts. In Proceedings of the 2023 CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing Systems (CHI ’23), April 23–28, 2023, Hamburg, Germany. ACM, New York, NY, USA, 21 pages.
d606a330-p002-c01 · PDF p. 2 · Original passage
Using this tool as a design probe, we observed how 10 participants without substantial prompt design experience executed a chatbot design task using BotDesigner, to explore a few diferent pieces of this larger question. Our fndings suggest that, while end-users can explore prompt designs opportunistically, they struggle to make robust, systematic progress. Their struggles echo many well-known struggles observed in end-user programming systems (EUPS) and non-expect users of interactive machine learning (iML) systems. Additional barriers to efective prompt design stem from limited conceptions of LLMs’ prompt understanding and execution abilities and their understandable inclinations to design prompts that resemble human-to-human instructions. We discuss these observations’ implications for designing efective end-user-facing LLM-based design tools, implications for education that improves LLM-andprompt literacy among programmers and the general public, and opportunities for further research. This paper makes three contributions.
From the paper
Core findings reported by the authors
The authors state that participants explored prompt designs opportunistically, not systematically, and that they struggled in ways echoing end-user programming and interactive machine learning systems. They also report that expectations from human-to-human instruction and a tendency to overgeneralize were barriers to effective prompt design. Their overview of later pages adds that most participants declared success after a single instance and rarely used systematic testing.

For your research
Relevance to your user study
Because the paper's probe was a prototype tool with 10 participants, a similar protocol (think-aloud, a clear target task, observing unassisted behaviour) could be a useful model for your study. Its findings on how users test and generalize prompts may help you define what behaviours to measure. Whether this fits your project depends on your specific research questions, which you have not yet described in detail.
d606a330-p002-c02 · PDF p. 2 · Original passage
This paper makes three contributions. First, it describes a novel, no-code LLM-and-prompt-based chatbot design tool that encourages iterative design and evaluation of robust prompt strategies (rather than opportunistic experimentation.) Second, it ofers a rare rich description of how non-experts intuitively approached prompt design, and where, how, and why they struggled. Finally, it identifes opportunities for non-expert-facing prompt design tools and open research questions in making LLM-powered design innovation accessible. 2 RELATED WORK 2.1 The Promises of Non-Expert Prompt Design Today’s chatbot design practice—i.e., designing multi-turn conversational interactions—follows a well-established workfow [12, 13, 24, 25, 39, 44]. Designers frst (i) identify the chatbot’s functionality or persona and draft ideal user-bot conversations, for example, through Wizard-of-Oz or having experts drafting scripts; (ii) create a dialogue fow template (e.g., “(1) greeting message; (2) questions to collect user intention; (3) ...
Limitation / uncertainty
Limitations to keep in mind
The authors note that the participant pool skewed toward STEM professionals and graduate students and is not representative, and that interviewer advice varied across participants. The retrieved passages do not include the full results, so details beyond the abstract and introduction are not yet confirmed here.

PaperLens reply (expand a source to see the cited text; highlighted = evidence quote that occurs in it)
Short answer
Not quite. In this paper the ten participants are not a held-out test set for evaluating the tool. They are a small sample of non-experts in a formative design-probe study, and their prompts were observed and analysed qualitatively, not scored as benchmark examples.
d606a330-p007-c03 · PDF p. 7 · Original passage
Participants. All participants ( = 10) had little to no experience with prompt design; none had substantial prior experience 2Regression tests, in the context of software engineering and in the sense we intend here, are tests that verify that any new changes to a system do not reintroduce previously-resolved errors (called regressions). working with LLMs. We recruited participants with varying levels of programming and chatbot design experience. Recall that our focus here is understanding end-users in the domain of prompt design; experience in chatbot design was thus not considered grounds for exclusion. Our sample size was chosen in line with prior work around formative testing for usability [19, 43]: our goal is to explore the frst, “easy-to-fnd” problems users encounter when engaged in our task, and our experience with a pilot user suggested that problem discoverability was likely to be high. Our participant pool, skewed towards professionals and graduate students in STEM-related felds, is not representative of the population at large.

d606a330-p009-c01 · PDF p. 9 · Original passage
Two of the authors then compared these approaches across participants and categorized their struggles using afnity diagrams [34] and by creating a service blueprint [5] that documents the specifc approaches individual participants attempt. We chose these methods from HCI and the feld of service design because our focus is on discovering opportunities created by a new technology, rather than understanding how an existing set of users engages in established work practices—a context in which a grounded theory approach would be more conventional. 4 USER STUDY FINDINGS We found the following: (1) Using BotDesigner, all participants were able to engage in ad hoc opportunistic iteration on prompts, with local testing of prompt changes. Two out of our ten participants required assistance in order to begin this process, but could then iterate on their own. (2) Participants’ struggles with generating prompts, evaluating prompt efectiveness, and explaining prompt efects, primarily stemmed from (a) over-generalization from single observations of success and failure of prompt changes; and (b) models of system behavior rooted in human-human interactions.
From the paper

The authors describe the study as formative: the sample size follows prior formative usability testing, and the goal was to explore the first, easy-to-find problems users encounter. They also state that the participant pool is not representative of the wider population.

For your research
Implications for your user study
Treating your participants' prompts as qualitative behavioural data (for example, how they debug, over-generalise, or evaluate changes) may fit your HCI goal better than treating them as evaluation examples. If you want to claim anything about how well prompts work, you would need a separate, predefined measure. The sample size and the sampling of this paper are also only a reference point for your own recruitment decisions.

PaperLens reply (expand a source to see the cited text; highlighted = evidence quote that occurs in it)
Short answer
The probe is not a baseline model. It is BotDesigner, a no-code prompt design tool the authors built as a research artifact to observe how non-experts approach prompt design. It was used to elicit their intuitive behaviour and thought processes, not to be compared against.
d606a330-p001-c00 · PDF p. 1 · Original passage
Why Johnny Can’t Prompt: How Non-AI Experts Try (and Fail) to Design LLM Prompts J.D. Zamfrescu-Pereira zamf@berkeley.edu UC Berkeley Berkeley, CA, USA Bjoern Hartmann bjoern@eecs.berkeley.edu UC Berkeley Berkeley, CA, USA ABSTRACT Pre-trained large language models (“LLMs”) like GPT-3 can engage in fuent, multi-turn instruction-taking out-of-the-box, making them attractive materials for designing natural language interactions. Using natural language to steer LLM outputs (“prompting”) has emerged as an important design technique potentially accessible to non-AI-experts. Crafting efective prompts can be challenging, however, and prompt-based interactions are brittle. Here, we explore whether non-AI-experts can successfully engage in “end-user prompt engineering” using a design probe—a prototype LLM-based chatbot design tool supporting development and systematic evaluation of prompting strategies. Ultimately, our probe participants explored prompt designs opportunistically, not systematically, and struggled in ways echoing end-user programming systems and interactive machine learning systems.
d606a330-p004-c01 · PDF p. 4 · Original passage
We chose to create a design probe because the robustness of prompt strategies is highly dependent on specifc conversational contexts, as is users’ ability to create prompts and debug. Enabling people to engage in designing LLM-and-prompt-based chatbots hands-on offers deeper insights than conducting interviews about hypothetical scenarios alone. We chose to purpose-build a prompt design tool because existing tools focus on enabling non-experts to experiment with various prompting strategies rather than crafting robust and generalizable prompts. 3.1 Designing a No-Code Prompt Design Tool as Probe Design Goals. We have two goals in designing BotDesigner: First, to enable end-users, without any prompt design experience or even programming or ML experience, to (i) create a chatbot using prompts, without code, and (ii) systematically evaluate their prompts. Second, to allow end-users to engage in this process in a fexible, open-ended way, allowing us to observe their intuitive behaviors and thought processes.
From the paper
What BotDesigner was
The authors describe BotDesigner as a no-code prompt design tool developed as a design probe, and the study combined it with contextual inquiry and interviews. Its stated goals were to let people without prompt design, programming, or ML experience create chatbots with prompts and systematically evaluate those prompts.
d606a330-p004-c00 · PDF p. 4 · Original passage

d606a330-p004-c01 · PDF p. 4 · Original passage

Second, to allow end-users to engage in this process in a fexible, open-ended way, allowing us to observe their intuitive behaviors and thought processes.
Explanation
Why it is a probe, not a baseline
A probe is a provocative prototype used to learn about people's behaviour, expectations, and struggles in a realistic task. A baseline would be a reference system whose performance is measured and compared with a new method. Here, the tool was the object of observation, and the analysis was qualitative, focused on opportunistic iteration and the reasons for failures.
d606a330-p004-c01 · PDF p. 4 · Original passage

We chose to purpose-build a prompt design tool because existing tools focus on enabling non-experts to experiment with various prompting strategies rather than crafting robust and generalizable prompts. 3.1 Designing a No-Code Prompt Design Tool as Probe Design Goals. Second, to allow end-users to engage in this process in a fexible, open-ended way, allowing us to observe their intuitive behaviors and thought processes.

For your research
Possible use in your study
If your user study has non-experts writing prompts for an LLM-based tool, a probe-style prototype could help you observe their intuitive strategies and misconceptions, rather than only asking about hypothetical scenarios. Whether this fits your design is your decision; you would need to decide whether your goal is to observe behaviour or to measure outcomes against a comparison condition.

PaperLens reply (expand a source to see the cited text; highlighted = evidence quote that occurs in it)
Short answer
The ten non-expert prompt designers were the full user-study sample; the three academic researchers in the pilot were a separate group used to validate the tool. The paper says two of the authors analysed the data qualitatively using affinity diagrams and service blueprints, but the retrieved passages do not say whether coding was independent or how agreement was checked.
d606a330-p007-c02 · PDF p. 7 · Original passage
It is worth noting that although BotDesigner enables this full workfow, we do not necessarily expect all end-users to engage in all its steps. However, we did validate BotDesigner’s functionality and usability throughout this workfow via a set of pilot user studies (see Appendix A.3 for details) to ensure that these pieces are available should users choose to systematically assess their prompt designs. 3.3 User Study Design To understand how end users intuitively approach prompt design, we invited 10 non-expert prompt designers to use BotDesigner and think-aloud. We invited the participants to improve upon a baseline chatbot template towards given goals (see Tasks below), while we observed and took note of the various prompt design approaches and testing strategies they engaged in. We chose the task of improving a chatbot, instead of creating it from scratch, in order to avoid the Blank Page Syndrome, the efect where users staring at a blank page do not know where to begin. Participants.
d606a330-p017-c02 · PDF p. 17 · Original passage
A.3 Pilot Evaluation of BotDesigner’s Use To validate that BotDesigner does enable systematic prompt evaluation, we recruited = 3 academic researchers with a range of interest levels and experience with conversational agent design4 and evaluated how efectively they could identify common or particularly severe bugs or errors in a baseline chatbot, and how efectively they could evaluate a new bot template for improvements over the baseline bot. In advance of the interview, we collected conversations with the baseline bot from AMT workers conversing with a baseline chatbot. We then asked our pilot users to (1) browse the collected conversations to fnd errors and label them with categorization tags; (2) evaluate a “new” template with updated prompts, provided 3When used to predict subsequent tokens given a specifed prefx (which we call a “prompt” in this paper), language models typically assign a probability to every possible subsequent token, and then select among the most likely contenders. The temperature parameter afects how the next prediction is selected among the probability-ranked tokens.
d606a330-p009-c01 · PDF p. 9 · Original passage
Two of the authors then compared these approaches across participants and categorized their struggles using afnity diagrams [34] and by creating a service blueprint [5] that documents the specifc approaches individual participants attempt. We chose these methods from HCI and the feld of service design because our focus is on discovering opportunities created by a new technology, rather than understanding how an existing set of users engages in established work practices—a context in which a grounded theory approach would be more conventional. 4 USER STUDY FINDINGS We found the following: (1) Using BotDesigner, all participants were able to engage in ad hoc opportunistic iteration on prompts, with local testing of prompt changes. Two out of our ten participants required assistance in order to begin this process, but could then iterate on their own. (2) Participants’ struggles with generating prompts, evaluating prompt efectiveness, and explaining prompt efects, primarily stemmed from (a) over-generalization from single observations of success and failure of prompt changes; and (b) models of system behavior rooted in human-human interactions.

The authors invited ten non-expert prompt designers to use BotDesigner and think aloud, improving a baseline chatbot template towards given goals. This is the main user study that the findings in the paper come from.

A.3 Pilot Evaluation of BotDesigner’s Use To validate that BotDesigner does enable systematic prompt evaluation, we recruited = 3 academic researchers with a range of interest levels and experience with conversational agent design4 and evaluated how efectively they could identify common or particularly severe bugs or errors in a baseline chatbot, and how efectively they could evaluate a new bot template for improvements over the baseline bot. d606a330-p017-c04 · PDF p. 17 · Original passage
We learned a few things from this small pilot that ultimately informed our probe. First, we learned that BotDesigner functioned well enough for a small set of researchers to use it successfully. Second, critically, we observed that looking over a set of prerecorded conversations is quite cognitively demanding, involving lots of context switching, because these were not conversations pilot users themselves had with the bot. As a result of these observations, we (1) retained the “Error Browser”, but (2) dropped a prototype “Conversation Browser” view (focused on tracking common conversational threads across conversations) from the version of BotDesigner we used in our probe. We also (3) designed a much more open-ended task for our probe requiring less knowledge of task minutiae to identify errors, and encouraging users to have their own conversations with the bot, while skipping the initial collection of conversations from AMT workers and thus avoiding the need for participants to understand the task well enough to identify subtle errors in instruction.
From the paper
How the data were analysed
Two of the authors compared approaches across participants and categorised their struggles using affinity diagrams and a service blueprint. The authors say they chose these HCI and service-design methods over grounded theory, which they describe as the more conventional approach for established work practices.

Paper: Hunyuan3D 2.0: Scaling Diffusion Models for High Resolution Textured 3D Assets Generation
Reading goal: Explore potential use in my research
Research context: 我是 computer vision 方向的 PhD，研究如何从网络上公开的照片生成可以用于 AR 的 3D assets。
Language setting: 中文（简体）

Turn 1

For A · previous PaperLens reply
(first message of the conversation)
Learner message
我想先了解一下大概流程。Hunyuan3D 2.0 是怎么从一张输入图片生成 3D shape 的？能不能先简单讲讲这个 shape generation 的主要步骤，我后面再看细节。
Move the simulated learner was given
Ask how the shape is generated from an input image.
PaperLens reply (expand a source to see the cited text; highlighted = evidence quote that occurs in it)
Short answer
简单说，Hunyuan3D 2.0 的 shape 部分分两步：先用 Hunyuan3D-ShapeVAE 把 3D 网格压缩成潜空间中的一串连续 token；再用基于 flow 的扩散 Transformer（Hunyuan3D-DiT）在这个潜空间里，根据输入图片预测 token 序列，最后由 VAE 解码器把 token 解码成多边形网格。
2e0d0789-p004-c01 · PDF p. 4 · Original passage
This design is driven by the successful applications of latent diffusion models in image and video generation. Specifically, our shape generation model consists of (1) an autoencoder – Hunyuan3D-ShapeV AE (Sec. 3.1) that compresses the shape of a 3D asset represented by polygon mesh into a sequence of continuous tokens in the latent space; (2) a flow-based diffusion model – Hunyuan3D-DiT (Sec. 3.2), trained on the latent space of ShapeV AE for predicting object token sequences from a user-provided image. The predicted tokens are further decoded into a polygon mesh with V AE decoder. The details of these models are illustrated below. 3.1 Hunyuan3D-ShapeV AE Hunyuan3D-ShapeV AE employs vector sets, a compact neural representation for 3D shapes proposed by 3DShape2VecSet [112], which has also been leveraged in the recent work Dora [11]. Followed by Michelangelo [119], we use a variational encoder-decoder transformer for shape compression and decoding.
2e0d0789-p003-c04 · PDF p. 3 · Original passage
The comparison shows the superiority of Hunyuan3D 2.0 in alignment between conditional images and generated meshes, generation of fine-grained details, and human preference ratings. 2 Hunyuan3D 2.0 Architecture In this section, we elaborate on the model architecture of Hunyuan3D 2.0, focusing on two main components: the shape generation model and the texture generation model. Fig. 2 illustrates the pipeline of Hunyuan3D 2.0 for creating a high-resolution textured 3D asset. Given an input image, Hunyuan3D-DiT initially generates a high-fidelity bare mesh via the shape generation model. This model comprises a Hunyuan3D-ShapeV AE and a Hunyuan3D-DiT, which will be discussed in Sec. 3. Subsequently, by leveraging strong geometric priors and the input image, we introduce Hunyuan3D- Paint as our texture generation model in Sec. 4. This model produces self-consistent multi-view outputs, which are used for baking high-definition texture maps.
From the paper
流程的三个主要环节
论文说 shape 生成模型由两部分组成：一是把 polygon mesh 压缩成潜空间连续 token 的自编码器，二是在该潜空间上训练、以用户图片为条件预测 token 序列的扩散模型；预测出的 token 再经 VAE 解码器还原为网格。论文同时指出，给定输入图片后，Hunyuan3D-DiT 先生成 bare mesh，之后才进入纹理生成阶段。

2e0d0789-p003-c04 · PDF p. 3 · Original passage
The comparison shows the superiority of Hunyuan3D 2.0 in alignment between conditional images and generated meshes, generation of fine-grained details, and human preference ratings. 2 Hunyuan3D 2.0 Architecture In this section, we elaborate on the model architecture of Hunyuan3D 2.0, focusing on two main components: the shape generation model and the texture generation model. Fig. 2 illustrates the pipeline of Hunyuan3D 2.0 for creating a high-resolution textured 3D asset. Given an input image, Hunyuan3D-DiT initially generates a high-fidelity bare mesh via the shape generation model. Explanation
用通俗的话理解这条流程
可以把它理解为：先学一个“压缩字典”（ShapeVAE），能把一个 3D 形状压成少量潜变量并再还原；然后学一个“按图作画”的生成器（DiT），在这些潜变量中，根据输入图片生成合适的潜变量；最后用字典把潜变量还原成网格。这种做法借鉴了图像和视频生成中成功的潜扩散模型思路。
2e0d0789-p004-c00 · PDF p. 4 · Original passage
Shape Generation Texture Synthesis Hunyuan3D-DiT Hunyuan3D-PaintHunyuan3D-ShapeVAE Image Delighting Reference Branch Generation Branch Multi-Task Attention Normal Position Cross Attention Self Attention Importance Uniform Cross Attention Self Attention x 8 Decoded Mesh FPS x 16 Q ··· ··· KV Latent Tokens ··· Marching Cube ··· ··· Grid Queries InputImage Delighted Image Single Image Super-Resolution Image Denoising Transformer Latent Tokens ··· Steps x N Baking Figure 2: An overall of Hunyuan3D 2.0 architecture for 3D generation. It consists of two main components: Hunyuan3D-DiT for generating bare mesh from a given input image and Hunyuan3D- Paint for generating a textured map for the generated bare mesh. Hunyuan3D-Paint takes geometry conditions – normal maps and position maps of generated mesh as inputs and generates multi-view images for texture baking. 3 Generative 3D Shape Generation Hunyuan3D 2.0 employs the architecture of the latent diffusion model [74, 112, 119, 114] for shape generation. This design is driven by the successful applications of latent diffusion models in image and video generation.
From the paper
论文中的关键设计
论文称 shape 生成采用潜扩散模型的架构，并说明这一设计受到潜扩散模型在图像和视频生成中成功应用的启发。在训练上，DiT 是在 ShapeVAE 的潜空间上，用 flow-matching 目标训练的 dual-single stream transformer。

Hunyuan3D-Paint takes geometry conditions – normal maps and position maps of generated mesh as inputs and generates multi-view images for texture baking. 3 Generative 3D Shape Generation Hunyuan3D 2.0 employs the architecture of the latent diffusion model [74, 112, 119, 114] for shape generation. This design is driven by the successful applications of latent diffusion models in image and video generation.
2e0d0789-p003-c02 · PDF p. 3 · Original passage
To this end, we presentHunyuan3D 2.0, a 3D asset creation system with two strong open-sourced 3D foundation models:Hunyuan3D-DiTfor generative shape creation andHunyuan3D-Paintfor generative texture synthesis. Hunyuan3D 2.0 features a two-stage generation pipeline, starting with the creation of a bare mesh, followed by the synthesis of a texture map for that mesh. This strategy is effective for decoupling the difficulties of shape and texture generation [ 34, 107, 46, 47] and also provides flexibility for texturing either generated or handcrafted meshes. With this architecture, our shape creation model – Hunyuan3D-DiT, is designed as a large-scale flow-based diffusion model. As a prerequisite, we first train an autoencoder – Hunyuan3D-ShapeV AE using advanced techniques such as mesh surface importance sampling and variational token length to capture fine-grained details on the meshes. Then, we build up a dual-single stream transformer [ 45] on the latent space of our V AE with the flow-matching [53, 24] objective.
For your research
对你的研究的参考价值
如果你的目标是从网络照片生成 AR 可用的 3D 资产，这条“单图 → 潜空间 token → 网格”的路线可以作为一个参考基线：它把形状生成与输入图片条件解耦，并且在潜空间里做扩散。你可以先思考：网络照片通常有遮挡、光照和背景干扰，而论文的 DiT 输入是用户提供的图片，这一点需要你自己判断是否适合你的数据。
2e0d0789-p004-c01 · PDF p. 4 · Original passage
This design is driven by the successful applications of latent diffusion models in image and video generation. Specifically, our shape generation model consists of (1) an autoencoder – Hunyuan3D-ShapeV AE (Sec. 3.1) that compresses the shape of a 3D asset represented by polygon mesh into a sequence of continuous tokens in the latent space; (2) a flow-based diffusion model – Hunyuan3D-DiT (Sec. 3.2), trained on the latent space of ShapeV AE for predicting object token sequences from a user-provided image. The predicted tokens are further decoded into a polygon mesh with V AE decoder. Followed by Michelangelo [119], we use a variational encoder-decoder transformer for shape compression and decoding.

2e0d0789-p004-c01 · PDF p. 4 · Original passage
This design is driven by the successful applications of latent diffusion models in image and video generation. Specifically, our shape generation model consists of (1) an autoencoder – Hunyuan3D-ShapeV AE (Sec. 3.1) that compresses the shape of a 3D asset represented by polygon mesh into a sequence of continuous tokens in the latent space; (2) a flow-based diffusion model – Hunyuan3D-DiT (Sec. 3.2), trained on the latent space of ShapeV AE for predicting object token sequences from a user-provided image. The predicted tokens are further decoded into a polygon mesh with V AE decoder. The details of these models are illustrated below. 3.1 Hunyuan3D-ShapeV AE Hunyuan3D-ShapeV AE employs vector sets, a compact neural representation for 3D shapes proposed by 3DShape2VecSet [112], which has also been leveraged in the recent work Dora [11]. Followed by Michelangelo [119], we use a variational encoder-decoder transformer for shape compression and decoding.

Short answer
可以这样理解：Hunyuan3D-DiT 只负责根据输入图片生成无纹理的几何网格（裸网格）；Hunyuan3D-Paint 则以这个网格的法线图和位置图为几何条件，并结合输入图片，生成多视角图像，再烘焙成纹理贴图。两者的衔接点就是 DiT 输出的网格。
2e0d0789-p003-c04 · PDF p. 3 · Original passage
The comparison shows the superiority of Hunyuan3D 2.0 in alignment between conditional images and generated meshes, generation of fine-grained details, and human preference ratings. 2 Hunyuan3D 2.0 Architecture In this section, we elaborate on the model architecture of Hunyuan3D 2.0, focusing on two main components: the shape generation model and the texture generation model. Fig. 2 illustrates the pipeline of Hunyuan3D 2.0 for creating a high-resolution textured 3D asset. Given an input image, Hunyuan3D-DiT initially generates a high-fidelity bare mesh via the shape generation model. This model comprises a Hunyuan3D-ShapeV AE and a Hunyuan3D-DiT, which will be discussed in Sec. 3. Subsequently, by leveraging strong geometric priors and the input image, we introduce Hunyuan3D- Paint as our texture generation model in Sec. 4. This model produces self-consistent multi-view outputs, which are used for baking high-definition texture maps.

2e0d0789-p003-c02 · PDF p. 3 · Original passage
To this end, we presentHunyuan3D 2.0, a 3D asset creation system with two strong open-sourced 3D foundation models:Hunyuan3D-DiTfor generative shape creation andHunyuan3D-Paintfor generative texture synthesis. Hunyuan3D 2.0 features a two-stage generation pipeline, starting with the creation of a bare mesh, followed by the synthesis of a texture map for that mesh. This strategy is effective for decoupling the difficulties of shape and texture generation [ 34, 107, 46, 47] and also provides flexibility for texturing either generated or handcrafted meshes. With this architecture, our shape creation model – Hunyuan3D-DiT, is designed as a large-scale flow-based diffusion model. As a prerequisite, we first train an autoencoder – Hunyuan3D-ShapeV AE using advanced techniques such as mesh surface importance sampling and variational token length to capture fine-grained details on the meshes. Then, we build up a dual-single stream transformer [ 45] on the latent space of our V AE with the flow-matching [53, 24] objective.

PaperLens reply (expand a source to see the cited text; highlighted = evidence quote that occurs in it)
Short answer
根据目前检索到的段落，论文只说明 Hunyuan3D-DiT 以一张输入图片为条件生成裸网格，但没有说明它对图片的背景、光照或遮挡有什么具体要求。Image Delighting 确实是为光照和阴影服务的，但它属于纹理阶段（Hunyuan3D-Paint）的预处理，目的是防止光照被烘焙进贴图，并不是 DiT 的输入处理，也不直接处理杂乱背景或遮挡。

2e0d0789-p007-c01 · PDF p. 7 · Original passage
To achieve these objectives, we employ a three-stage framework, including a pre-processing stage (Sec. 4.1), a multi-view image synthesis stage (Sec. 4.2, Hunyuan3D-Paint), and a texture baking stage based on dense multi-view inference (Sec. 4.3). Other details related to model training and textand image-to-texture pipeline are provided in Sec. 4.4. Fig. 5 illustrates the complete pipeline of our texture map synthesis method. 4.1 Pre-processing Image Delighting Module.The reference image typically exhibits pronounced and varied illumination and shadow, whether collected by the user or generated by T2I models. Directly inputting such images into the multi-view generation framework can cause illumination and shadows to be baked into the texture maps. To address this issue, we leverage a delighting procedure on the input image via an image-to-image approach [6] before multi-view generation. Specifically, to train such an image delighting model, we collect a large-scale 3D dataset and render it under the illumination of a random HDRI environmental map and an even white light to form the corresponding pair-wise image data.
From the paper
DiT 的输入条件：论文说了什么

From the paper
Image Delighting 解决的是纹理阶段的光照问题
论文指出，用户收集或 T2I 模型生成的参考图通常有明显且多变的光照和阴影，直接输入多视角生成框架会让光照和阴影被烘焙进纹理贴图。因此 Image Delighting 是在多视角生成之前对输入图做的处理，服务于纹理。这一段描述的是 Paint 部分，而不是 DiT 的输入。

Other details related to model training and textand image-to-texture pipeline are provided in Sec. 4.4. Ask in a broad way what this article is about.
PaperLens reply (expand a source to see the cited text; highlighted = evidence quote that occurs in it)
Short answer
這篇論文是 Miguel Sicart 的〈Defining Game Mechanics〉，主要在提出一個「遊戲機制（game mechanics）」的正式定義：機制是由「代理者（agents）」調用、為與遊戲狀態互動而設計的方法，並借用物件導向程式設計的概念。作者希望這個定義能用於形式化分析遊戲，並連結控制器設計與使用者體驗，最後以 Shadow of the Colossus、Rez 與 Every Extend Extra 進行比較分析。
f17ba500-p001-c04 · PDF p. 1 · Original passage
In this article, I propose a definition of game mechanics useful for the analysis of games and their formal constituents. This definition will allow for formalized analysis of game structures, and it will also open up for the possibility of connecting formal game analysis with research on controller designs and user experience. I define game mechanics, using concepts from object-oriented programming, as methods invoked by agents, designed for interaction with the game state. With this formalized definition, I intend to: Provide a tool to discover, describe, and interrelate game mechanics in any given game. Define mechanics also in relation to elements of the game system, game hardware and player experience, mapping mechanics to input procedures and player emotions. Even though I will be mentioning concepts like game rules, challenges, emotions and user experience, it is not my intention to enter the debate on those topics. Here, I use those concepts in a relational way: defining game mechanics requires mentioning and acknowledging rules, challenges and emotions.
ov-1 · PDF p. 1-1 · PaperLens-generated overview summary (not the authors' words)
The article by Miguel Sicart addresses the ambiguity of the term 'game mechanics' in game research and design, using the cover system in Gears of War and the minimalist game Orbital as opening examples. It proposes a formal definition that treats game mechanics, drawing on object-oriented programming concepts, as methods invoked by agents for interacting with the game state, defined in relation to rules, challenges and emotions used in an instrumental way. The introduction states the aims: to provide an analytical tool for describing game mechanics, to connect formal analysis with controller design and user experience, and to set up a comparative analysis of Shadow of the Colossus, Rez and Every Extend Extra. The pages also outline a plan that includes a review of previous definitions of mechanics, a discussion of primary and secondary mechanics, and a conclusion on the definition's shortcomings, though these later sections are not included in the text shown.
From the paper
論文要解決的問題
作者指出「遊戲機制」一詞在遊戲研究與設計中定義含混，並以 Gears of War 的掩體機制與極簡遊戲 Orbital 作為開場例子。作者認為既有定義缺乏足夠精確性，難以用於形式分析，特別是難以連結規則與機制，也難以辨識個別機制。
ov-1 · PDF p. 1-1 · PaperLens-generated overview summary (not the authors' words)
The article by Miguel Sicart addresses the ambiguity of the term 'game mechanics' in game research and design, using the cover system in Gears of War and the minimalist game Orbital as opening examples. It proposes a formal definition that treats game mechanics, drawing on object-oriented programming concepts, as methods invoked by agents for interacting with the game state, defined in relation to rules, challenges and emotions used in an instrumental way. The introduction states the aims: to provide an analytical tool for describing game mechanics, to connect formal analysis with controller design and user experience, and to set up a comparative analysis of Shadow of the Colossus, Rez and Every Extend Extra. The pages also outline a plan that includes a review of previous definitions of mechanics, a discussion of primary and secondary mechanics, and a conclusion on the definition's shortcomings, though these later sections are not included in the text shown.
ov-2 · PDF p. 2-2 · PaperLens-generated overview summary (not the authors' words)
These pages review how game studies has analysed games through their formal elements, distinguishing systems, players, and player-system contexts. They trace the distinction between rules and mechanics from Avedon through Lundgren and Björk, Rouse, Fullerton et al., Cook, the MDA framework, and Järvinen. The authors argue that most prior definitions of game mechanics lack the precision needed for formal analysis, particularly in linking rules to mechanics and identifying individual mechanics. The pages also note that Järvinen's goal-oriented account struggles to explain sandbox play in games such as Sim City and Crackdown.
From the paper
核心定義與論文架構
作者把機制定義為「由代理者調用、為與遊戲狀態互動而設計的方法」，並主張規則與機制在本體上有所區別：規則定義可能性空間並規範狀態轉換，機制則是互動方法。論文依序回顧既有定義、提出正式定義、討論核心／主要／次要機制，最後以三款遊戲作比較分析，並在結論反思定義的不足。
f17ba500-p004-c05 · PDF p. 4 · Original passage
For designers and theorists, game mechanics are discrete units that can be created, analyzed and put in relation to others. But for any agent in a game, the mechanics is everything that affords agency in the game world. Mechanics is thus tied to agency in the game system. With this definition of game mechanics, I have intended to contribute to game studies by: Formalizing an ontological difference between rules and mechanics that can potentially lead to detailed game analysis, and Suggesting a mapping between game mechanics, input procedures, and player experience. This very formal definition still leaves some questions unanswered, especially with regards to well-known terminology such as core mechanics. In the next section, I present some further implications of this definition for the analysis of games. Interlude: Core, Primary and Secondary Mechanics Game design literature uses the "game mechanics" concept extensively, incorporating certain qualifiers to it. It is not rare to find in the literature notions like "core mechanics" (Järvinen, 2998, p. 255; Rollings and Adams, 2007, pp.
ov-3 · PDF p. 3-3 · PaperLens-generated overview summary (not the authors' words)
These pages define game mechanics as methods invoked by agents, designed for interaction with the game state, borrowing terminology from object-oriented programming. The author argues this definition is less anthropocentric than prior approaches, since mechanics can be invoked by human players, AI agents, or bots, with examples drawn from Shadow of the Colossus and Gears of War. The text also argues that the framework supports mapping mechanics to input devices, using UML-style modeling, and discusses how fighting-game mastery relates to input mapping. Finally, it introduces 'context mechanics' triggered by the player's situation in the game world, illustrated with Gears of War, Assassin's Creed and Wario Ware, Inc.; the author notes that studying bots' access to mechanics is outside the article's scope.
ov-4 · PDF p. 4-4 · PaperLens-generated overview summary (not the authors' words)
These pages from Sicart's article 'Defining Game Mechanics' argue for an ontological distinction between rules and mechanics: rules provide the possibility space and regulate state transitions, while mechanics are methods designed for interaction with the game state. The author illustrates this with Shadow of the Colossus, where climbing is a mechanic constrained by a stamina property derived from a rule, and the game loop evaluates interactions against those rules. The text also distinguishes designed gameplay from players' unpredictable uses, such as toyplay, noting that formal design cannot fully determine how a game is played. Finally, it begins an interlude on how qualifiers like core, primary and secondary mechanics fit the proposed definition, drawing on Salen and Zimmerman and Järvinen.
ov-6 · PDF p. 6-6 · PaperLens-generated overview summary (not the authors' words)
These pages come from a Game Studies article defining game mechanics, shifting from formal definition to application. The author compares Shadow of the Colossus, Rez and Every Extend Extra through their core input-based mechanics, arguing that each uses a tension-and-release structure. The stabbing mechanic in Shadow of the Colossus, the shoot mechanic in Rez and the explode mechanic in Every Extend Extra are read as design choices that shape intended player experiences such as powerlessness and epic achievement, or synaesthetic musical engagement. The section concludes that developers modified well-known core mechanics to alter player expectations and agency.
From the paper
對實際應用的意義
作者認為此定義可協助描述與修改機制、改善遊戲設計文件，並促進設計師與程式設計師之間的溝通。作者也承認此定義並非終極定義，仍有許多灰色地帶未處理。
ov-7 · PDF p. 7-7 · PaperLens-generated overview summary (not the authors' words)
These pages conclude Sicart's article defining game mechanics as methods invoked by agents for interacting with the game world, drawing on object-oriented programming and game studies. They discuss practical uses of the definition: formally describing and modifying mechanics, improving game design documentation, and easing communication between designers and programmers. The conclusion argues that mechanics are performative while rules are normative, and that treating them as distinct is useful for analysing computer games. The author acknowledges that the definition is not the ultimate one, that many grey areas remain unexplored, and that no dominant definition of rules or mechanics exists in game studies. The pages end with acknowledgements and a reference list.
f17ba500-p007-c02 · PDF p. 7 · Original passage
Conclusion This article was born out of necessity: having an analytical vocabulary for defining game structures and systems that allowed a formal, precise, and scalable description of games as systems and how they interrelate with player practices. The result of this necessity is a formal definition of game mechanics that owes to object-oriented programming its formal phrasing, while inheriting from game studies the figure of players, or agents, as fundamental to understand how games are designed and played. This article has defined game mechanics as methods invoked by agents for interacting with the game world. This definition allows the study of the systemic structure of games in terms of actions afforded to agents to overcome challenges, but also the analysis of how actions are mapped onto input devices and how mechanics can be used to create specific emotional experiences in players. There are, however, many grey areas I do not have the space to focus on here. Perhaps the most significant is the ontological distinction between rules and mechanics. Game researchers have argued convincingly that mechanics could be understood as subsets of rules.

若只想大致了解，可先讀前言與結論，掌握「機制是代理者調用的互動方法」這個核心想法；若有興趣的是實際應用，可再看第三部分對三款遊戲的比較分析。
f17ba500-p001-c05 · PDF p. 1 · Original passage
Here, I use those concepts in a relational way: defining game mechanics requires mentioning and acknowledging rules, challenges and emotions. I do so in an instrumental way and leave for further research the implications of this definition for understanding other systemic components of games. Since both game researchers and game designers have covered the topic of game mechanics, I begin this article with an analytical summary of the major works on this topic, providing a general overview of these previous definitions of game mechanics and place my work within this tradition. The second part of this article presents the definition of game mechanics, detailing the elements that compose it. I then present a brief reflection on primary and secondary mechanics and how they can be derived from this definition. These concepts are put into practice in the third part, where I perform a comparative analysis of Shadow of the Colossus (Team Ico, 2005), Rez (United Game Artists, 2002), and Every Extend Extra (Q Entertainment, 2006), highlighting the use of this concept of mechanics in the research on game structure and user experience.

Learner message
好，那我想再確認一下，作者到底是怎麼定義「遊戲機制」的？我看到你說是『代理者調用的方法』，那是不是就是玩家按按鈕、做動作的那些東西？

Ask how the author defines a game mechanic.
PaperLens reply (expand a source to see the cited text; highlighted = evidence quote that occurs in it)
Short answer
大致可以這樣理解，但不完全等同於「玩家按按鈕」。作者把遊戲機制定義為「由代理者調用、為與遊戲狀態互動而設計的方法」。代理者不只是人類玩家，電腦系統或 AI（例如 bots）也可以是代理者；機制是代理者在規則所劃定的可能性空間中能採取的行動。按鍵與輸入裝置的對應，是論文另外討論的「機制與輸入程序、玩家體驗之間的對應」。
f17ba500-p003-c00 · PDF p. 3 · Original passage
to player agency and game rules. In the following section I present a formal definition of game mechanics, together with the arguments that make it a more precise and inclusive approach than those reviewed in this section. Defining Game Mechanics Let's start with a definition: game mechanics are methods invoked by agents, designed for interaction with the game state. The different components of this definition require further explanation: "Methods invoked by agents" defines this approach to game mechanics, as it formalizes the use of terminology taken from the object oriented programming paradigm (Weisfeld, 2000). In this appropriation of the terminology, object orientation provides a set of metaphors that describe the elements of systems and their interrelations. I do not want to imply that the analysis of the source code of a game will reveal that all game mechanics have been implemented as methods of classes or that object-oriented programming should be considered a default methodology for the actual production of computer games. Nor am I implying that the Object Oriented Framework should be extended to a formal analysis of all elements of the game.
f17ba500-p003-c03 · PDF p. 3 · Original passage
For example, in Shadow of the Colossus we find the following mechanics: to climb, ride (the horse), stab, jump, shoot (arrows), whistle, grab, run (and variations like swim or dive). In Gears of War, a noncomprehensive list would be: cover, shoot, reload, throw (grenade), look (at a point of interest), use, give orders, switch weapons[2]. All of these are methods for agency within the game world, actions the player can take within the space of possibility created by the rules. This definition departs from the implicit anthropocentrism of previous approaches. Game mechanics can be invoked by any 2026/9/5 15:03 Game Studies - Defining Game Mechanics https://gamestudies.org/0802/articles/sicart 5/16 agent, be that human or part of the computer system. For instance, AI agents also have a number of methods available to interact with the gameworld. On occasion, those methods will be other than the ones made available to the human player, which can have consequences worth of analysis. This approach can be particularly interesting when trying to understand the effect of bots in MMORPGs, since bots are agents that optimize their interaction by focusing on a core set of mechanics.

From the paper
機制與規則、代理者的關係
作者指出，規則提供機制可以發生的可能性空間，規則是為代理者而建模，機制則是為代理者的行動而設計。對一般代理者而言，機制就是所有能讓它在遊戲世界中行動的東西。
f17ba500-p004-c00 · PDF p. 4 · Original passage
Implicit in this definition is an ontological difference between rules and mechanics. Game mechanics are concerned with the actual interaction with the game state, while rules provide the possibility space where that interaction is possible, regulating as well the transition between states. In this sense, rules are modeled after agency, while mechanics are modeled for agency. In this object oriented framework, rules could be considered general or particular properties of the game system and its agents. All objects in games have properties. These properties are often either rules or determined by rules. These rules are evaluated by a game loop, an algorithm that relates the current state of the game and the properties of the objects with a number of conditions that consequently can modify the game state. For example, the winning condition, the losing condition and the effects of action in the player's avatar health are calculated when running the game loop. This algorithm relates rules with mechanics, exemplifying the applicability of an ontological distinction between rules and mechanics.

Explanation
用一個比喻幫助理解
可以把遊戲想像成一個系統，「機制」像是系統裡可以被呼叫的動作，例如跳躍、射擊；「代理者」則是呼叫這些動作的人或電腦程式。按鍵只是讓人類玩家觸發動作的一種方式，論文關心的是動作本身，以及它如何影響遊戲狀態。這是我的說明，論文並未用按鈕來比喻。

PaperLens reply (expand a source to see the cited text; highlighted = evidence quote that occurs in it)
Short answer
不完全是。論文把「規則」和「機制」區分開來：規則提供可能性空間並規範狀態之間的轉換，機制則是與遊戲狀態的實際互動。你的理解接近論文提到的一種舊觀點（將機制視為規則系統的一部分），但作者認為區分兩者有助於分析遊戲。
f17ba500-p004-c00 · PDF p. 4 · Original passage
Implicit in this definition is an ontological difference between rules and mechanics. Game mechanics are concerned with the actual interaction with the game state, while rules provide the possibility space where that interaction is possible, regulating as well the transition between states. In this sense, rules are modeled after agency, while mechanics are modeled for agency. In this object oriented framework, rules could be considered general or particular properties of the game system and its agents. All objects in games have properties. These properties are often either rules or determined by rules. These rules are evaluated by a game loop, an algorithm that relates the current state of the game and the properties of the objects with a number of conditions that consequently can modify the game state. For example, the winning condition, the losing condition and the effects of action in the player's avatar health are calculated when running the game loop. This algorithm relates rules with mechanics, exemplifying the applicability of an ontological distinction between rules and mechanics.
f17ba500-p002-c01 · PDF p. 2 · Original passage
11). Some formalist approaches makes a difference between the rules of the game and the actions afforded to players by those rules. This conceptual perspective can be tracked back to Avedon (1971) who suggests a formal structure of games in which there are "specific operations, required courses of action, method of play," which he defines as the "procedure for action", as opposed to the "rules governing action", which are "fixed principles that determine conduct and standards for behavior" (p. 422). However, this formal distinction between rules and mechanics is not always applied in game mechanics research. Lundgren and Björk (2003) define game mechanics as "any part of the rule system of a game that covers one, and only one, possible kind of interaction that takes place during the game, be it general or specific (...) mechanics are regarded as a way to summarize game rules". In this view, mechanics is a term that encompasses those rules that are applied when the player interacts with the game, and there is no need for a definitional distinction between rules and mechanics. Game mechanics would be low-level descriptions of game rules or clusters of game rules.
From the paper
論文如何區分規則與機制
作者指出，機制關注的是與遊戲狀態的實際互動，而規則提供互動得以發生的可能性空間，並規範狀態之間的轉換。作者因此說規則是依據能動性（agency）建模的，機制則是為能動性而建模的。作者也指出，規則具有規範性，機制則具有表演性（performative）。

f17ba500-p007-c03 · PDF p. 7 · Original passage
Perhaps the most significant is the ontological distinction between rules and mechanics. Game researchers have argued convincingly that mechanics could be understood as subsets of rules. However, rules are normative, while mechanics are performative, and I have argued that this ontological distinction can be extremely beneficial for the analysis of computer games. Game studies history shows that there is no dominant definition of key concepts like rules or mechanics, and that those that attempted have yet to succeed. This article should not be read as the ultimate definition of game mechanics. This definition is 2026/9/5 15:03 Game Studies - Defining Game Mechanics https://gamestudies.org/0802/articles/sicart 13/16 flawed, yet less so than some previous ones. My goal will be achieved if I have succeeded in communicating to the reader one simple notion: that it is possible and useful to understand game mechanics as different from game rules, and in that understanding, we can more clearly describe how games can be designed to affect players in unprecedented ways.
From the paper
你的理解與論文提到的另一種觀點
論文提到，有些研究（如 Lundgren 和 Björk）把機制視為規則的一部分，也就是低層次的規則描述或規則群集，因此不需要在規則與機制之間做定義上的區分。作者並不同意把兩者完全等同，而是主張區分有助於更清楚地描述遊戲分析。
f17ba500-p002-c01 · PDF p. 2 · Original passage
11). Some formalist approaches makes a difference between the rules of the game and the actions afforded to players by those rules. This conceptual perspective can be tracked back to Avedon (1971) who suggests a formal structure of games in which there are "specific operations, required courses of action, method of play," which he defines as the "procedure for action", as opposed to the "rules governing action", which are "fixed principles that determine conduct and standards for behavior" (p. 422). However, this formal distinction between rules and mechanics is not always applied in game mechanics research. Lundgren and Björk (2003) define game mechanics as "any part of the rule system of a game that covers one, and only one, possible kind of interaction that takes place during the game, be it general or specific (...) mechanics are regarded as a way to summarize game rules". In this view, mechanics is a term that encompasses those rules that are applied when the player interacts with the game, and there is no need for a definitional distinction between rules and mechanics. Game mechanics would be low-level descriptions of game rules or clusters of game rules.
Illustrative example · not from the paper
用規則手冊與實際行動來理解（舉例，非論文內容）
可以想像一本遊戲的規則手冊：它說明哪些行動可行、哪些狀態會導致勝負。機制則像是玩家在這套規則允許的範圍內，實際執行的互動，例如在《Shadow of the Colossus》中攀爬、跳躍或射箭。這個比喻只是幫助理解，論文本身並未使用這個例子。

PaperLens reply (expand a source to see the cited text; highlighted = evidence quote that occurs in it)
Short answer
簡單說，論文把「規則」看成劃定可能性空間、規範狀態如何轉換的框架，「機制」則是實際與遊戲狀態互動的部分。規則回答「什麼可以發生」，機制回答「實際上怎麼互動」。
f17ba500-p004-c00 · PDF p. 4 · Original passage
Implicit in this definition is an ontological difference between rules and mechanics. Game mechanics are concerned with the actual interaction with the game state, while rules provide the possibility space where that interaction is possible, regulating as well the transition between states. In this sense, rules are modeled after agency, while mechanics are modeled for agency. In this object oriented framework, rules could be considered general or particular properties of the game system and its agents. All objects in games have properties. These properties are often either rules or determined by rules. These rules are evaluated by a game loop, an algorithm that relates the current state of the game and the properties of the objects with a number of conditions that consequently can modify the game state. For example, the winning condition, the losing condition and the effects of action in the player's avatar health are calculated when running the game loop. This algorithm relates rules with mechanics, exemplifying the applicability of an ontological distinction between rules and mechanics.
Illustrative example · not from the paper
用足球比賽來理解
這是一個不是來自論文的例子：足球的規則包括「用手碰球犯規」「越位不算進球」等，它們規定了比賽中允許什麼、狀態如何改變。機制則像球員實際在場上的傳球、跑位和射門，也就是在這些規則框架內與比賽狀態互動的實際行為。

f17ba500-p003-c03 · PDF p. 3 · Original passage
For example, in Shadow of the Colossus we find the following mechanics: to climb, ride (the horse), stab, jump, shoot (arrows), whistle, grab, run (and variations like swim or dive). In Gears of War, a noncomprehensive list would be: cover, shoot, reload, throw (grenade), look (at a point of interest), use, give orders, switch weapons[2]. All of these are methods for agency within the game world, actions the player can take within the space of possibility created by the rules. This definition departs from the implicit anthropocentrism of previous approaches. Game mechanics can be invoked by any 2026/9/5 15:03 Game Studies - Defining Game Mechanics https://gamestudies.org/0802/articles/sicart 5/16 agent, be that human or part of the computer system. For instance, AI agents also have a number of methods available to interact with the gameworld. On occasion, those methods will be other than the ones made available to the human player, which can have consequences worth of analysis. This approach can be particularly interesting when trying to understand the effect of bots in MMORPGs, since bots are agents that optimize their interaction by focusing on a core set of mechanics.
f17ba500-p007-c02 · PDF p. 7 · Original passage
Conclusion This article was born out of necessity: having an analytical vocabulary for defining game structures and systems that allowed a formal, precise, and scalable description of games as systems and how they interrelate with player practices. The result of this necessity is a formal definition of game mechanics that owes to object-oriented programming its formal phrasing, while inheriting from game studies the figure of players, or agents, as fundamental to understand how games are designed and played. This article has defined game mechanics as methods invoked by agents for interacting with the game world. This definition allows the study of the systemic structure of games in terms of actions afforded to agents to overcome challenges, but also the analysis of how actions are mapped onto input devices and how mechanics can be used to create specific emotional experiences in players. There are, however, many grey areas I do not have the space to focus on here. Perhaps the most significant is the ontological distinction between rules and mechanics. Game researchers have argued convincingly that mechanics could be understood as subsets of rules.

f17ba500-p003-c04 · PDF p. 3 · Original passage
This approach can be particularly interesting when trying to understand the effect of bots in MMORPGs, since bots are agents that optimize their interaction by focusing on a core set of mechanics. This design choice may lead to an imbalance in the game system, in terms of its dynamics or its economy. Another extension of this approach would draw a distinction between agents in a game with mechanics and agents without access to mechanics. For example, some bots do have access to mechanics while other game agents do not have access to mechanics and hence cannot interact with the game state. This line of research, however, is outside the scope of this article. The second advantage is that it eases the mapping of mechanics to input devices, allowing for a great degree of granularity in the analysis of games. Applying the conceptual framework of Object Oriented programming determines that an agent invokes a mechanic in order to interact with the game[3]. When it comes to players, input devices - from mouse and keyboard to the Wii Fit Board - mediate this process. In Gears of War, the cover mechanic is invoked by pressing the A button in the controller.

f17ba500-p003-c00 · PDF p. 3 · Original passage
to player agency and game rules. In the following section I present a formal definition of game mechanics, together with the arguments that make it a more precise and inclusive approach than those reviewed in this section. Defining Game Mechanics Let's start with a definition: game mechanics are methods invoked by agents, designed for interaction with the game state. The different components of this definition require further explanation: "Methods invoked by agents" defines this approach to game mechanics, as it formalizes the use of terminology taken from the object oriented programming paradigm (Weisfeld, 2000). In this appropriation of the terminology, object orientation provides a set of metaphors that describe the elements of systems and their interrelations. I do not want to imply that the analysis of the source code of a game will reveal that all game mechanics have been implemented as methods of classes or that object-oriented programming should be considered a default methodology for the actual production of computer games. Nor am I implying that the Object Oriented Framework should be extended to a formal analysis of all elements of the game.

f17ba500-p003-c02 · PDF p. 3 · Original passage
A game mechanic, then, is the action invoked by an agent to interact with the game world, as constrained by the game rules. In Gears of War, if the player wants to take cover, she has to press the A button in the controller. This will make the avatar seek cover in the closest environment object that can provide that cover. In that sense, a mechanic is limited by the rules that apply to the gameworld (the general physics simulations, for instance, whose objects are suitable for providing some kind of cover), and, on occasion, to rules that apply exclusively to that particular mechanic - for example, some mechanics can only be invoked in certain environments or gameplay contexts. Following Järvinen (2008), the best way of understanding mechanics as methods is to formalize them as verbs, with other syntactical/structural elements, such as rules, having influence on how those verbs act in the game. For example, in Shadow of the Colossus we find the following mechanics: to climb, ride (the horse), stab, jump, shoot (arrows), whistle, grab, run (and variations like swim or dive).
f17ba500-p003-c03 · PDF p. 3 · Original passage
For example, in Shadow of the Colossus we find the following mechanics: to climb, ride (the horse), stab, jump, shoot (arrows), whistle, grab, run (and variations like swim or dive). In Gears of War, a noncomprehensive list would be: cover, shoot, reload, throw (grenade), look (at a point of interest), use, give orders, switch weapons[2]. All of these are methods for agency within the game world, actions the player can take within the space of possibility created by the rules. This definition departs from the implicit anthropocentrism of previous approaches. Game mechanics can be invoked by any 2026/9/5 15:03 Game Studies - Defining Game Mechanics https://gamestudies.org/0802/articles/sicart 5/16 agent, be that human or part of the computer system. For instance, AI agents also have a number of methods available to interact with the gameworld. On occasion, those methods will be other than the ones made available to the human player, which can have consequences worth of analysis. This approach can be particularly interesting when trying to understand the effect of bots in MMORPGs, since bots are agents that optimize their interaction by focusing on a core set of mechanics.

Following Järvinen (2008), the best way of understanding mechanics as methods is to formalize them as verbs, with other syntactical/structural elements, such as rules, having influence on how those verbs act in the game. For example, in Shadow of the Colossus we find the following mechanics: to climb, ride (the horse), stab, jump, shoot (arrows), whistle, grab, run (and variations like swim or dive).
f17ba500-p003-c03 · PDF p. 3 · Original passage
For example, in Shadow of the Colossus we find the following mechanics: to climb, ride (the horse), stab, jump, shoot (arrows), whistle, grab, run (and variations like swim or dive). In Gears of War, a noncomprehensive list would be: cover, shoot, reload, throw (grenade), look (at a point of interest), use, give orders, switch weapons[2]. All of these are methods for agency within the game world, actions the player can take within the space of possibility created by the rules. This definition departs from the implicit anthropocentrism of previous approaches. Game mechanics can be invoked by any 2026/9/5 15:03 Game Studies - Defining Game Mechanics https://gamestudies.org/0802/articles/sicart 5/16 agent, be that human or part of the computer system. For instance, AI agents also have a number of methods available to interact with the gameworld. On occasion, those methods will be other than the ones made available to the human player, which can have consequences worth of analysis. This approach can be particularly interesting when trying to understand the effect of bots in MMORPGs, since bots are agents that optimize their interaction by focusing on a core set of mechanics.
PaperLens inference
用這個定義判斷跳躍
如果把「跳躍」理解為玩家（代理者）調用的動作，它符合機制的定義。至於「只能在地面或某些條件下跳」這類限制，則比較接近論文所說會約束機制的規則，例如論文用 stamina 門檻限制攀爬的例子。
f17ba500-p004-c01 · PDF p. 4 · Original passage
This algorithm relates rules with mechanics, exemplifying the applicability of an ontological distinction between rules and mechanics. For example, in Shadow of the Colossus players have a game mechanic called "climb", but they are also determined by a property called "stamina", which is the algorithmic translation of a rule: "players have x stamina units". The climbing mechanic states that when invoked, stamina is lost at a certain ratio. A property/rule states that if stamina is below a certain threshold, climbing is not possible anymore. The game loop checks the game state; if the player invokes the climb mechanic, those functions that determine the consequences and boundaries of the players' interaction are called, and the resulting changes in the game state are evaluated against the rules of the game. Then, the player will succeed or not in "climbing", depending on their "stamina". The second part of the definition claims that game mechanics are methods "designed for interaction with the game state". This implies that the task of game designers is to create mechanics that agents can use to interact with the game. These interactions modify the game state (Juul, 2005, 59-64).
f17ba500-p003-c02 · PDF p. 3 · Original passage
A game mechanic, then, is the action invoked by an agent to interact with the game world, as constrained by the game rules. In Gears of War, if the player wants to take cover, she has to press the A button in the controller. This will make the avatar seek cover in the closest environment object that can provide that cover. In that sense, a mechanic is limited by the rules that apply to the gameworld (the general physics simulations, for instance, whose objects are suitable for providing some kind of cover), and, on occasion, to rules that apply exclusively to that particular mechanic - for example, some mechanics can only be invoked in certain environments or gameplay contexts. Following Järvinen (2008), the best way of understanding mechanics as methods is to formalize them as verbs, with other syntactical/structural elements, such as rules, having influence on how those verbs act in the game. For example, in Shadow of the Colossus we find the following mechanics: to climb, ride (the horse), stab, jump, shoot (arrows), whistle, grab, run (and variations like swim or dive).

假設角色只能在踩到地面時起跳，「按下跳躍鍵後角色跳起」是機制，「必須在地面上才能起跳」則是規則。這只是用來幫助理解的假設例子，並非論文內容。

Move the simulated learner was given
Ask why participants struggled to generalize from examples, according to the paper.
PaperLens reply (expand a source to see the cited text; highlighted = evidence quote that occurs in it)
Short answer
According to the retrieved passages, the paper does not attribute the struggle mainly to too few examples. It points to participants over-generalizing from single data points and limited experience, plus expectations carried over from human-to-human instruction. Some participants also could not see how examples from one domain applied to their own task.
d606a330-p010-c00 · PDF p. 10 · Original passage
CHI ’23, April 23–28, 2023, Hamburg, Germany Zamfirescu-Pereira, Wong, Hartmann & Yang the “retry” button on the specifc problematic utterance the participant was trying to resolve, or by starting new conversations. 4.2 Challenges in End-User Prompt Design Though we report on a wide spectrum of our participants’ struggles iterating on prompts, we found that many of these behaviors arose from two fundamental sources: over-generalization from limited experience, and a social lens that fltered participants’ prompts (and the system’s responses to those prompts) through expectations originating in human-human interactions. First, participants over-generalized from single data points, whether positive or negative. For example, when writing a prompt to generate specifc behavior, participants often stopped iterating once the behavior was observed in a single conversational context, without considering other conversations or contexts—or gave up too early if the behavior was not observed on a frst or second attempt, and assumed the system was incapable of generating that behavior.
d606a330-p011-c05 · PDF p. 11 · Original passage
The universality of the advice “show, don’t tell” [46] for novice writers, suggesting that humans commonly reach for descriptions over examples, is another possible corroboration for this bias. Seeking Help Two participants (P2, P4) who did seek solutions on the web both had a hard time making use of the examples they found, in part because they could not see how specifc prompt designs translated across domains. P2, for example, searched Google for “how can GPT- 3 assume an identity, ” hoping to fnd advice on having their chatbot take on Carla’s identity. Despite fnding an example illustrating how to generate text written in the style of a particular author, P2 decided this example was too far from what they were looking for, and did not attempt that particular approach. One participant, P6, didn’t pursue seeking solutions on the web because “I have no idea what I would Google because I’ve never worked with language models before. ” (P6) 4.3.2 Evaluating Prompts. Although all participants struggled with generating prompts in various ways, they were all ultimately successful in adding and modifying prompt text in ways that led to some, if not all, of the desired efects.
From the paper
What the paper says about the cause
The authors describe two fundamental sources of participants' struggles: over-generalization from limited experience, and a social lens that filtered prompts through expectations from human-human interactions. They report that participants over-generalized from single data points, whether positive or negative, often stopping once a behavior appeared in one conversation.

Limitation / uncertainty
Your training-set hypothesis
The passages describe the problem as participants' tendency to overgeneralize from limited experience, not as the number of examples provided. Whether a larger or smaller set of examples changes behavior is not addressed in these passages, so the training-set framing is your hypothesis rather than the paper's finding.

For your research
Implications for your user study
You could separate two factors in your design: how many examples participants see, and whether they are prompted to test a behavior across several conversations before concluding. Measuring how often participants stop after a single observation would test the over-generalization account directly, and cross-domain examples could be varied to test transfer.

Short answer
The passages point to how participants thought about the examples, not to how many examples they saw. They over-generalized from single data points, and their expectations from human-to-human instruction led them to favor direct instruction over examples. Some also could not see how an example from one domain applied to their own task. The passages do not test whether more examples would have helped.

Learner message
Ok, I understand better now, thank you. So the design probe in this paper is like a baseline model, right? Like the participants are the test set and the probe is what we compare the prompts against?
Move the simulated learner was given
Make a wrong analogy: ask whether the design probe is a kind of baseline model.
PaperLens reply (expand a source to see the cited text; highlighted = evidence quote that occurs in it)
Short answer
Not quite. In this paper, the design probe is a research instrument, not a baseline model. BotDesigner is a no-code prompt design tool that the authors built so they could watch how non-experts intuitively approach prompt design. The participants are not a test set, and the passages do not describe comparing their prompts against the probe.
d606a330-p004-c01 · PDF p. 4 · Original passage
We chose to create a design probe because the robustness of prompt strategies is highly dependent on specifc conversational contexts, as is users’ ability to create prompts and debug. Enabling people to engage in designing LLM-and-prompt-based chatbots hands-on offers deeper insights than conducting interviews about hypothetical scenarios alone. We chose to purpose-build a prompt design tool because existing tools focus on enabling non-experts to experiment with various prompting strategies rather than crafting robust and generalizable prompts. 3.1 Designing a No-Code Prompt Design Tool as Probe Design Goals. We have two goals in designing BotDesigner: First, to enable end-users, without any prompt design experience or even programming or ML experience, to (i) create a chatbot using prompts, without code, and (ii) systematically evaluate their prompts. Second, to allow end-users to engage in this process in a fexible, open-ended way, allowing us to observe their intuitive behaviors and thought processes.
d606a330-p003-c05 · PDF p. 3 · Original passage
3 METHOD: DESIGN PROBE In this work, we aim to understand how non-experts intuitively approach designing robust prompts, and whether and how they struggle in doing so. We want to dive deep into what those struggles reveal about people’s assumptions and understanding of promptbased systems in order to inform how end-user-facing prompt design tools might best support their users. We chose to investigate these questions in the context of designing instructional chatbots—chatbots that walk users through an activity (e.g., cooking a recipe, fxing a wif connection) while answering user questions and engaging in social conversation as needed. Chatbot tasks are sufciently open-ended that they open the door to a wide variety of types of problems and prompt-based solution approaches. Instructional chatbots represent a common chatbot use case (e.g., customer service bots), and their constituent tasks are some of the tasks that GPT-3 peforms more efectively out-of-the-box.
From the paper
What the probe is for
The authors say a hands-on tool gives deeper insight than interviews about hypothetical scenarios, and that an open-ended design lets them observe participants' intuitive behaviors and thought processes. The probe is therefore a way to elicit and study behaviour, and the study's aim is to understand how non-experts approach designing robust prompts and where they struggle.

Explanation
Why the baseline/test-set analogy does not fit
A baseline model and a test set are used to measure performance on a fixed task. Here, the probe sets up a task in which people's behaviour is observed and interpreted qualitatively, with the authors' findings about over-generalization and human-human expectations emerging from that observation. The passages describe no benchmark or comparison of prompts against the probe.
d606a330-p009-c01 · PDF p. 9 · Original passage
Two of the authors then compared these approaches across participants and categorized their struggles using afnity diagrams [34] and by creating a service blueprint [5] that documents the specifc approaches individual participants attempt. We chose these methods from HCI and the feld of service design because our focus is on discovering opportunities created by a new technology, rather than understanding how an existing set of users engages in established work practices—a context in which a grounded theory approach would be more conventional. 4 USER STUDY FINDINGS We found the following: (1) Using BotDesigner, all participants were able to engage in ad hoc opportunistic iteration on prompts, with local testing of prompt changes. Two out of our ten participants required assistance in order to begin this process, but could then iterate on their own. (2) Participants’ struggles with generating prompts, evaluating prompt efectiveness, and explaining prompt efects, primarily stemmed from (a) over-generalization from single observations of success and failure of prompt changes; and (b) models of system behavior rooted in human-human interactions.
For your research
What this suggests for your user study
If you use a probe-like tool, it may be better to treat it as an instrument that shapes and reveals users' prompting behaviour, not as a reference system. The paper also notes that the sample is small, skewed toward STEM professionals and students, and not representative, so you would need to think about how your own participant sampling and any comparisons would be designed. You would need to decide yourself whether a probe fits your research questions.
d606a330-p007-c03 · PDF p. 7 · Original passage
Participants. All participants ( = 10) had little to no experience with prompt design; none had substantial prior experience 2Regression tests, in the context of software engineering and in the sense we intend here, are tests that verify that any new changes to a system do not reintroduce previously-resolved errors (called regressions). working with LLMs. We recruited participants with varying levels of programming and chatbot design experience. Recall that our focus here is understanding end-users in the domain of prompt design; experience in chatbot design was thus not considered grounds for exclusion. Our sample size was chosen in line with prior work around formative testing for usability [19, 43]: our goal is to explore the frst, “easy-to-fnd” problems users encounter when engaged in our task, and our experience with a pilot user suggested that problem discoverability was likely to be high. Our participant pool, skewed towards professionals and graduate students in STEM-related felds, is not representative of the population at large.
